A practical algorithm for 2 2 -admissibility
Christine Awofeso, Patrick Greaves, Oded Lachish, Felix Reidl

Abstract
The 2 2 -admissibility of a graph is a promising measure to identify real-world networks which have an algorithmically favourable structure. In contrast to other related measures, like the weak/strong 2 2 -colouring numbers or the maximum density of graphs that appear as 1 1 -subdivisions, the 2 2 -admissibility can be computed in polynomial time. However, so far these results are theoretical only and no practical implementation to compute the 2 2 -admissibility exists. Here we present an algorithm which decides whether the 2 2 -admissibility of an input graph G G is at most p p in time O ⁡ ( p 4 ​ | V ⁡ ( G ) | ) O(p^{4}|V(G)|) and space O ⁡ ( | E ⁡ ( G ) | + p 2 ) O(|E(G)|+p^{2}) . The simple structure of the algorithm makes it easy to implement. We evaluate our implementation on a corpus of 214 real-world networks and find that the algorithm runs efficiently even on networks with millions of edges, that it has a low memory footprint, and that indeed many networks have a small 2 2 -admissibility.

1
Introduction

Our work here is motivated by efforts to apply algorithms from sparse graph
theory to real-world graph data, in particular algorithms that work
efficiently if certain
sparseness measures
of the input graph are
small. In algorithm theory, specifically from the purview of parametrized algorithms, this approach has been highly successful: by designing algorithms
around sparseness measures like treewidth
[
5
,
2
,
25
]
,
maximum degree
[
28
]
, the size of an excluded minor
[
6
]
, or the size of a ‘shallow’ excluded minor
[
21
,
12
]
, many hard problems become fixed-parameter tractable, meaning that they can be solved in time
f(k,\xi)n^{O(1)}
for some function
f
, where
\xi
is the sparseness measure and
k
the problem parameter (e.g. solution size).

For real-world applications, we find a tension regarding sparseness measures: if a measure is too restrictive, it will be large for most graphs in practice. If it is too permissive, then its does not provide enough leverage to design fast algorithms. We aim to identify measures which strike a balance, meaning measures that are small on many real-world networks
and
provide an algorithmic benefit. Additionally, we would like to be able to compute such measures efficiently.

A good starting point here is the
degeneracy
measure, which captures the maximum density of all subgraphs.
Specifically, a graph is
d
-degenerate if its vertices can be ordered in such a way that every vertex
x
has at most
d
neighbours that are smaller than
x
. Such orderings cannot exist for e.g. graphs that have a high minimum degree or contain big cliques as subgraphs. For most real-world graphs, however, the degeneracy is indeed small and can be computed quickly. In a survey of 206 networks from various domains by Drange
et al
.
[
9
]
, the degeneracy averaged about 23 with a median of 9 (we also replicated this data, see notes on experimental data below). Further,
small degeneracy can improve certain computational tasks significantly. For
example, counting all cliques in a
d
-degenerate graph
G
is possible in
time
O(d3^{d/3}|V(G)|)

[
14
]
while in general
this problem is
\#W[1]
-hard
[
15
]
1
1

1

This means that counting cliques of size
k
is, modulo some
complexity-theoretic assumption, not possible in time
f(k)n^{O(1)}
for
any function
f
.
. While we will use subgraph counting as a running example, many other types of problems become tractable on degenerate graphs
(see e.g.
[
23
,
9
,
16
,
24
]
).

However, degeneracy has clear limitations. Assume, for example, that we want to count how often a graph
H
appears as a subgraph in another graph
G
. Let
H^{(1)}
and
G^{(1)}
be the graphs obtained from
H
and
G
, respectively, by subdividing each edge once. Counting
H^{(1)}
in
G^{(1)}
is then the same as counting
H
in
G
, except that
G^{(1)}
is now
2
-degenerate. Therefore counting
H^{(1)}
in
2
-degenerate graphs must be hard
2
2

2

Here we specifically mean
\#W[1]
-hard, but other hardness results probably translate as well via this simple reduction
if counting
H
in general graphs is.
Using stronger complexity-theoretic assumptions, namely the
triangle counting conjecture
3
3

3

There exists a constant
\epsilon
such that finding a triangle in a graph cannot be done in time less than
O(m^{1+\epsilon-o(1)})
.

, Bera, Pashanasangi, and Seshadhri
[
3
]
showed that counting subgraphs with six or more vertices in
d
-degenerate graphs is not possible in time
o(|E(G)|^{1+\epsilon}f(d))
, where
\epsilon>0
stems from the conjecture, thus ruling out linear-time algorithms in this setting.

This leaves us with the question whether a sparseness measure exists that

1.

is small in (many) real-world networks,

2.

allows us to solve problems which remain hard on degenerate graphs,

3.

is computable with few resources, and

4.

can be computed exactly.

We have already provided our motivations for the first two points; the third point derives from our goal to make this measure usable for practical applications on graphs with millions of edges.
The last point warrants some further justification. Our effort of bringing theoretical algorithms into practice means that we need to
identify domains in which the ‘sparseness measures approach’ could provide more efficient algorithms, since the domains inform what type of problems are of interest. This task is much easier with a sparseness measure whose value is known exactly than with a sparseness measure whose value can only be approximated or computed heuristically.

We propose that the
2
-admissibility
has the above four properties. As a rough idea, if the degeneracy measures the maximum density of subgraphs in a host graph, the
2
-admissibility measures the maximum density of graphs that appear as
\leq 1
-subdivisions
4
4

4

A
\leq 1
-subdivision of a graph
H
is obtained by subdividing some edges of
H
one time.
in the host graph.
We postpone a deeper justification to Section
3
, since it involves quite a few technical details, but suffices to say that in contrast to other comparable measures, we know that the
2
-admissibility
can be computed exactly in polynomial time
[
10
]
. Further, we know from previous results
[
26
]
that graphs like
C_{6}
,
W_{6}
, or
K_{5,5}
can be counted in linear time in graphs with bounded
2
-admissibility, but not in degenerate graphs due to the above mentioned lower bounds by Bera
et al
.

Theoretical contribution.

We present a simple algorithm which decides whether the
2
-admissibility of
an input graph
G
is at most
p
in time
O(p^{4}|V(G)|)
using
O(|E(G)|+p^{2})
space. This improves on a previous algorithm described by Dvořák
[
10
]
with running time
O(|V(G)|^{2p+4})
and even beats the
2
-approximation with running time
O(|V(G)|^{3})
described in the same paper when
p<\sqrt{n}
. Dvořák also provides a theoretical linear-time algorithm for
2
-admissibility in
bounded expansion
classes (which include e.g. planar graphs, bounded-degree graphs and classes excluding a minor or topological minor; see Section
3
for a definition), however this algorithm relies on a data structure for dynamic first-order model checking
[
13
]
which certainly is not practical. Our algorithm runs in linear time as long as the
2
-admissibility is a constant, this includes graph classes that do not have bounded expansion.

We designed our algorithm with three goals in mind: The algorithm should have a very low memory footprint (as this is the more strictly constrained resource), it should be easy to implement, and it should be designed ‘optimistically’, meaning that it should only perform work if strictly necessary. We believe that we reached all three goals. On the flip-side, this means that the analysis of the algorithm is more involved.

Implementation and experiments.

We implemented our algorithm in Rust and tested it on a collection of 214 real-world networks, ranging in size from hundreds of edges to 11 million edges. The source code, test data and experimental results can be found at
https://github.com/chrisateen/admissibillity-rust
. Experimental results are also attached in the Appendix of this paper.

We ran experiments on a relatively modest machine, proving that our algorithm is indeed very resource efficient. Moreover, our results show that many real-world networks indeed have a small
2
-admissibility.

2
Preliminaries

In this paper all graphs are simple.

†
†
margin:

|G|
,
\|G\|
,
\operatorname{mm}(G)

For a graph
G
we use
V(G)
and
E(G)
to refer to its vertex set and edge set,
respectively. We use the short-hands
|G|\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|V(G)|
and
\|G\|\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|E(G)|
. We call a matching between two sets
X,Y

maximum
if it is the largest possible matching and
maximal
if the matching cannot be increased by adding an edge. We write
\operatorname{mm}(G)
to denote the maximum matching number of
G
.

†
†
margin:

x_{1}Px_{\ell}
, avoids

For sequences of vertices
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
, in particular paths, we use notations like
x_{1}Px_{\ell}
,
x_{1}P
and
Px_{\ell}
to denote the subsequences
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
,
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}
and
x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
, respectively.
Note that further on we sometimes refer to paths as having a

start-point
and an
end-point
, despite the fact that they are not directed. We do so to simplify the reference to the vertices involved.
A path
P

avoids
a vertex set
L
if no interior vertex of
P
is contained in
L
. Note that we allow both endpoints to be in
L
.

†
†
margin:

N_{L}
,
N_{R}
,
T^{2}_{L}

In the following, we will maintain a partition
L\cup R=V(G)
. Given these two sets, we will write
N_{L}(v)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=N(v)\cap L
and
N_{R}(v)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=N(v)\cap R
for
v\in G
. Moreover, we will often need the set of vertices
T^{2}_{L}(v)
which can be reached from
v
via paths of length two that avoid
L
and are not in
N(v)
, that is,

T^{2}_{L}(v)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{x\in V(G)\setminus N(v)\mid N_{R}(x)\cap N(v)\neq\emptyset\}
.

†
†
margin:

\mathbb{G}
,
<_{\mathbb{G}}

In Section
3
we will need to define the notion of an
ordered graph
. Given a graph
G
and a total ordering
<
on its vertex set, we define the ordered graph
\mathbb{G}=(G,<)
. We treat
\mathbb{G}
as a graph to allow notations like
E(\mathbb{G})
,
V(\mathbb{G})
etc. Given an
ordered graph
\mathbb{G}
, we write
<_{\mathbb{G}}
to denote its ordering relation. We imagine the graph ordered from left (small) to right (large) and will use ‘left’ and ‘right’ to talk about relations between vertices, e.g. we say ‘
u
lies left of
v
’ to mean
u<_{\mathbb{G}}v
.

†
†
margin:

\min_{<_{\mathbb{G}}}
,
N^{-}({\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}})
,
L_{{\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}}}

For a vertex set
X
of
\mathbb{G}
, we write

\min_{<_{\mathbb{G}}}X
to denote the smallest (leftmost) vertex in
X
according to
<_{\mathbb{G}}
. For a vertex
u\in V(\mathbb{G})
we write
N^{-}(u):=\{v\in N(u)\mid v<_{\mathbb{G}}u\}
for the
left neighbourhood
,
i.e. all neighbours of
u
that came before
u
in the ordering. Finally, for
u\in V(\mathbb{G})
we define the
\emph{prefixset}

L_{u}
as the set of all vertices
w\in V(\mathbb{G})
such that
w<_{\mathbb{G}}u
.

3
Admissibility and related measures

The
2
-admissibility (then just ‘admissibility’) was originally defined in by Kierstead and Trotter
[
18
]
in their study of planar graph colourings.
The more general notion of
r
-admissibility
was introduced by Dvořák
[
10
]
. Strictly speaking,
r
-admissibility is a family of measures where
r
governs how ‘deep’ into the graph we look. We present here the general definition first and then return to the case
r=2
.

†
†
margin:

(r,X)
-path packing

An
(r,X)
-path packing rooted at
v
is a family of paths
\mathcal{P}
with the following properties:

1.

Every path in
\mathcal{P}
has length at most
r
,

2.

all paths have
v
as start-point and a vertex of
X
as an end-point,

3.

all paths are vertex-disjoint with the exception of
v
, and

4.

all paths in
\mathcal{P}
avoid
X
.

†
†
margin:

\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{X}

We write
\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{X}(v)
to denote the maximum size of a
(r,X)
-path packing rooted at
v
.

†
†
margin:

r
-admissibility

The
r
-admissibility
\operatorname{adm}_{r}(\mathbb{G})
of an ordered graph
\mathbb{G}
is now defined as
5
5

5

Note that some authors define the admissibility as
\max_{v\in V(\mathbb{G})}\operatorname{pp}_{L_{v}}^{r}(v)+1
to align with other measures.

\displaystyle\operatorname{adm}_{r}(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle:=\max_{u\in V(\mathbb{G})}\operatorname{pp}_{L_{u}}^{r}(u),

recall that
L_{u}
is the prefix set of all vertices left of
u
in
\mathbb{G}
.
The
r
-admissibility
\operatorname{adm}_{r}(G)
of a graph
G
is the minimum of
\operatorname{adm}_{r}(\mathbb{G})
taken over all orderings
\mathbb{G}
of
G
.

†
†
margin:

strong and weak colouring

Two closely related measures are the
strong
r
-colouring number

\operatorname{scol}_{r}
and the
weak
r
-colouring number

\operatorname{wcol}_{r}
. Both are based on measuring the size of certain ‘reachable’ sets from every vertex in an ordered
graph
\mathbb{G}
. Specifically, we definde the
weakly
r
-reachable
set
W_{\mathbb{G}}^{r}(u)
and the
strongly
r
-reachable set

S_{\mathbb{G}}^{r}(u)
as

\displaystyle S^{r}_{\mathbb{G}}(u)

\displaystyle=\{v<_{\mathbb{G}}u\mid\exists~\text{$u$-$v$-path~$P$ of length at most~$r$ such that}~u=\min_{<_{\mathbb{G}}}(P\setminus v)\}

\displaystyle W^{r}_{\mathbb{G}}(u)

\displaystyle=\{v<_{\mathbb{G}}u\mid\exists~\text{$u$-$v$-path~$P$ of length at most~$r$ such that}~v=\min_{<_{\mathbb{G}}}P\}

In words, the vertices
v
that are strongly
r
-reachable from
u
lie to the left of
u
and are connected to
u
via a path of length
\leq r
that only uses vertices larger than
u
, with the exception of
v
itself. The vertices
that are weakly
r
-reachable from
u
also lie to the left of
u
, but the paths conneting them to
u
are now allowed to use vertices right of
v
.

The example below should help tease apart these definitions. Depicted are the left neighbourhood
N^{-}(x)
of
x
, a maximum
2
-path packing rooted at
x
, the set of strongly
2
-reachable vertices
S^{2}(x)
, and the set of weakly

2
-reachable vertices
W^{2}(x)
.

The two measure
\operatorname{scol}_{r}
and
\operatorname{wcol}_{r}
of an ordered graph
\mathbb{G}
are now defined, respectively, as

\operatorname{scol}_{r}(\mathbb{G}):=\max_{u\in V(\mathbb{G})}|S^{r}(u)|\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{wcol}_{r}(\mathbb{G}):=\max_{u\in V(\mathbb{G})}|W^{r}(u)|.

For an unordered graph
G
, the colouring numbers
\operatorname{scol}_{r}(G)
and
\operatorname{wcol}_{r}(G)
are again defined as the minimum over all possible orderings
\mathbb{G}
of
G
.

All three measures are generalizations of the degeneracy. Specifically, for
r=1
we have that
S^{1}(u)=W^{1}(u)=N^{-}(u)
and
\operatorname{pp}^{1}_{L_{u}}=|N^{-}(u)|
, therefore
\operatorname{adm}_{1}(\mathbb{G})=\operatorname{scol}_{1}(\mathbb{G})=\operatorname{wcol}_{1}(\mathbb{G})
and all three measures equal the degeneracy.
For
r\geq 2
, the measures are usually different. However, they
are closely related in the sense that for any ordered graph
\mathbb{G}
and any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\operatorname{adm}_{r}(\mathbb{G})\leq\operatorname{scol}_{r}(\mathbb{G})\leq\operatorname{wcol}_{r}(\mathbb{G})\leq(r^{2}\operatorname{adm}_{r}(\mathbb{G}))^{r},

,
where the first two inequalities follow quite straightforward from the definitions and the last inequality is due to Dvořák
[
11
]
(improving on an earlier bound
by the same author
[
10
]
). Note that the above inequalities also hold if we replaced the ordered graph with an unordered graph.

†
†
margin:

bounded expansion

For theoretical considerations, the three measures are often exchangable since if one is bounded, the other two are as well. The weak colouring number provides the most ‘structure’ to work with and has been used to great success in the study of
bounded expansion
classes (see e.g.
[
8
,
1
,
17
,
27
]
) as well as for designing algorithms
[
26
,
10
]
. The original definition of bounded expansion classes involves the notion of shallow minors
[
22
]
which we will forgo here, since Zhu
[
29
]
showed that bounded expansion classes are exactly those classes in which the weak
r
-colouring number is bounded for
any

r
, i.e. there exists some function
f
such that for every member
G
of the class it holds that
\operatorname{wcol}_{r}(G)\leq f(r)
. Note that we can equivalently use
\operatorname{adm}_{r}
or
\operatorname{scol}_{r}
in this definition since the measures bound each other.

However, both the weak and strong
2
-colouring numbers are
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to compute exactly
[
4
]
and for that reason, studies on practical values of these measures
[
20
,
7
]
are somewhat inconclusive as large values on some networks might simply be a failure of the respective heuristic
6
6

6

Some approximation algorithms exist, but the ratios are not good enough to paint a clear picture
.
The same holds true for comparable measures like the maximum density of
graphs that appear as
1
-subdivisions
[
19
]
which also bounds and is bounded by the
2
-admissibility. So for the purpose of finding an
exact
sparseness measure that can be computed efficiently, only the
2
-admissibility is a suitable candidate.

We close this section by showing a few basic properties of admissibility and path-packings.

Lemma 1
.

For every graph
G
and integers
p,r\geq 1
, if
\|G\|>p|G|
then
\operatorname{adm}_{r}(G)>p
.

Proof.

If
\|G\|>p|G|
, then for every order
<
of the graph
G
, there exists a vertex
v
such that
|L_{v}|>p
and hence
\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{L_{v}}(v)>p
.
∎

Lemma 2
.

Let
G
be a graph,
L\subseteq V(G)
,
v\in L
, and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
be a matching between the vertices of
N_{R}(v)
and of
T^{2}_{L}(v)
.
Then,
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\geq|N_{L}(v)|+|M_{v}|
and equality holds if
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
is a maximum matching.

Proof.

Fix
L
to be an arbitrary subset of
V(G)
and
v
an arbitrary vertex in
L
.
Let
R=V(G)\setminus L
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
a matching between the vertices of
N_{R}(v)
and of
T^{2}_{L}(v)
.

We show first that
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\geq|N_{L}(v)|+|M_{v}|
.
Afterwards we show that, if
M_{v}
is a maximum matching, then
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\leq|N_{L}(v)|+|M_{v}|
.
The lemma follows.

Let
\hat{\ell}
be the number of edges of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
and
\{x_{i}y_{i}\}_{i\in[\hat{\ell}]}
be the edges of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
with
x_{i}\in N_{R}(v)
and
y_{i}\in T^{2}_{L}(v)
. Construct the paths
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{vu\mid u\in N_{L}(v)\}\cup\{vx_{i}y_{i}\mid i\in[\hat{\ell}]\}
. Since the sets
N_{R}(v)
,
N_{L}(v)
and
T^{2}_{L}(v)
are all pairwise disjoint and the paths
vx_{i}y_{i}
only intersect in
v
, we conclude that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a
(2,L)
-path packing of size
|M_{v}|+|N_{L}(v)|
. Thus, by definition,
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\geq|M_{v}|+|N_{L}(v)|
.

Suppose that
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
is a maximum matching.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
(2,L)
-path packing rooted at
v
of size
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)
.
Let
\ell
be the number of paths of
\mathcal{P}
that have length one. Note that the remaining
s\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|\mathcal{P}|-\ell

paths all have length two and take the form
vx_{i}y_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq s
, where
x_{i}\in N_{R}(v)
and
y_{i}\in T^{2}_{L}(v)
.
By definition, all the paths of length one of
\mathcal{P}
have an end point in
N_{L}(v)
and hence
s\leq|N_{L}(v)|
.
Also by definition, all the
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are distinct, therefore the edges
\{x_{i}y_{i}\}_{i\in[s]}
are a matching between
N_{R}(v)
and of
T^{2}_{L}(v)
. Thus,
s
is at most the size of a maximum matching in
G
between the vertices of
N_{R}(v)
and of
T^{2}_{L}(v)
.
Consequently, as
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)=s+\ell
, it holds that
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\leq|N_{L}(v)|+|M_{v}|
.
∎

Lemma 3
.

Let
G
be a graph.
For every
L^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
,
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
v\in L^{\prime}
it holds that
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\leq\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L^{\prime}}(v)
.

Proof.

Let
L^{\prime},L
and
v
be selected arbitrarily so that
L^{\prime}\in V(G)
,
L\subset L^{\prime}
and
v\in L^{\prime}
.
Let
R=V(G)\setminus L
and
R^{\prime}=V(G)\setminus L^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
(2,L)
-path packing rooted at
v
of size
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)
.
By definition,
\mathcal{P}
contains all the vertices in
N_{L}(v)
and a maximum matching
\mathsf{M}
of size
s
between the vertices of
N_{R}(v)
and of
T^{2}_{L}(v)
.
Let
\{x_{i}y_{i}\}_{i\in[s]}
be the edges of
\mathsf{M}
with
x_{i}\in N_{R}(v)
and
y_{i}\in T^{2}_{L}(v)
.

Let
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}^{\prime}
be a matching that contains exactly every edge
x_{i}y_{i}
of
\mathsf{M}
such that
x_{i}\in N_{R^{\prime}}(v)
and
y_{i}\in N_{L^{\prime}}^{2}(v)
.
We notice that, as
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
, every vertex
y_{i}
in an edge of
\mathsf{M}
is also in
L^{\prime}
.
So, the only reason an edge
x_{i}y_{i}
of
\mathsf{M}
will not be in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}^{\prime}
is if
x_{i}\in L^{\prime}
(and not in
R^{\prime}
) which in turn means it is in
N_{L^{\prime}}(v)
.
Thus,
N_{L^{\prime}}(v)
contains all the vertices
x_{i}
such that
x_{i}y_{i}
is an edge in
\mathsf{M}
but not in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}^{\prime}
.

N_{L^{\prime}}(v)
also contains all the vertices of
N_{L}(v)
, since
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
.
Consequently, (*)
|N_{L^{\prime}}(v)|+\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}^{\prime}\|\geq|N_{L}(v)|+\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}\|
.
By Lemma
2
,
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L^{\prime}}(v)\geq|N_{L^{\prime}}(v)|+\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}^{\prime}\|
and
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)=|N_{L}(v)|+\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}\|
and therefore, by (*),
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L^{\prime}}(v)\geq\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)
.
∎

4
The theoretical algorithm

†
†
margin:

Main

We present here the Main Algorithm (
Main
).
The input to the algorithm is a graph
G
and a parameter
p
.
We assume that
\|G\|\leq pn
, since this can easily be checked and if it does not hold, then by Lemma
1
, the
2
-admissibility number of
G
is strictly larger than
p
.

Let us for now assume that we have access to an Oracle that, given a subset
L
of
V(G)
, can provide us with a vertex
v\in L

such that
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\leq p
if such a vertex exists. With the help of this Oracle, the following greedy algorithm returns an ordering
<
such that
\operatorname{adm}_{2}(G,<)\leq p
if one exists and otherwise returns FALSE:

1.

Initialise
L\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=V(G)
and
i=|G|
, as well as the Oracle.

2.

Find
v\in L
such that
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)\leq p
using the Oracle. If no such vertex exists return FALSE.

3.

Remove
v
from
L
, set
v_{i}
to be
v
and decrease
i
by one. Go to the previous step unless
L=\emptyset
.

4.

Return the order
v_{1},\ldots,v_{|G|}
.

The justification for why this greedy strategy works is a well-known property of
r
-admissibility, captured by the following lemma.

Lemma 4
.

A graph
G
has
r
-admissibility
p
, if and only if, for every non-empty
U\subseteq V
, there exists a vertex
u\in U
such that
\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{V\setminus U}(u)\leq p
.

Proof.

Suppose first that for every non-empty
U\subseteq V
, there exists a vertex
u\in U
such that
\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{V\setminus U}(u)\leq p
.
Then, an
r
-admissible order of
G
can be found as follows, first
initialise the set
U
to be equal to
V
and
i
to
|G|
, and then repeat the following two steps until
U
is empty: (1) find a vertex
u\in U
such that
\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{V\setminus U}(u)\leq p
removing it from
U
and adding it in the
i
’th place of the order
(2) decrease
i
by
1
.

We note that by construction, the
r
-admissibility of the order we got is at most
p
.
Thus,
G
has
r
-admissibility
p
.

Suppose that
G
has
r
-admissibility
p
.
Then, there exists an ordering,
<
of
V(G)
such that
\operatorname{adm}_{r}(G,<)\leq p
.
Let
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots u_{|G|}
be the vertices of
G
and suppose
that for every
i,j\in[|G|]
, such that
i<j
, it holds that
u_{i}<u_{j}
.

Let
u_{k}\in U
be the vertex with the maximum index in
U
.
We note that
U\subseteq L_{u_{k}}
.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\operatorname{pp}^{r}_{U}(u_{k})>p
.
Then, there exists an
(r,U)
-path packing
Pack
rooted at
u_{k}
of size
p+1
.
By definition, every path in
Pack
starts in
u_{k}
and ends in a vertex in

U
.

Thus, since
U\subseteq L_{u_{k}}
, every path
P
in
Pack

either avoids
L_{u_{k}}
, or includes a vertex from
L_{u_{k}}
.
If the second case holds for such a path
P
, then it has sub-path that
starts in
u_{k}
and ends in a vertex in
L_{u_{k}}
and avoids
L_{u_{k}}
.
Now, we can construct a new
(r,L_{u_{k}})
-path packing rooted at
u_{k}
with the same size as
Pack
(
p+1
), by iterating over all paths
P
and taking them if the avoid
L_{u_{k}}
and otherwise taking their subpath that avoid
L_{u_{k}}
.
since
(G,<)
has
r
-admissibility at most
p
.
∎

4.1
The Oracle

In the following, the set
L
is the set of all the vertices that the Oracle has yet to return and the set
R
is equal to
V(G)\setminus L
.

We note that, beyond providing the Oracle with the graph
G
and parameter
p
, there is no need to update the Oracle since it has the knowledge of the vertex it returned. To answer the queries efficiently, the Oracle maintains additional information for each vertex which enables it to answer queries in an amortized running time that is polynomial in
p
.
Specifically, the Oracle maintains a set of vertices
\mathsf{Cand}
(short for ‘candidates’), and the following data structures for each vertex
v\in L
:

N_{L}(v)
,
N_{R}(v)

The sets
N_{L}(v)
and
N_{R}(v)
as defined above

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}

A matching
(L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v},R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v},E^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v})

with
R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}\subseteq N_{R}(v)
,

L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}\subseteq T^{2}_{L}(v)
, and

matching edges
E^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}

While technically
N_{L}(v)
and
N_{R}(v)
are data structures distinct from the neighbourhood they represent, the Oracle will always update these data structures first to ensure consistency. For that reason, we avoid introducing additional notation and will simply equivocate the data structure and the neighbourhood it represents.
The Oracle has three types of operations:

Data structures initialisation.

Given
G
and
p
the Oracle initialises
\mathsf{Cand}
to be the set of all vertices of degree at most
p
in
G
and, for every
v\in V(G)
, it sets
N_{L}(v)=N_{G}(v)
,
N_{R}(v)=\emptyset
, and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}(v)
to be the empty graph.

Return vertex.

The Oracle returns a vertex from the set
\mathsf{Cand}
if it is not empty and otherwise it returns FALSE.

Update data structures.

The goal of the update is to ensure that the following conditions hold after a vertex is returned (removed from
L
). The conditions will be enforced by the algorithm
1
which runs just before the next vertex is returned:

[(1)]

1.

\mathsf{Cand}
contains all vertices
u\in L
with
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(u)\leq p
,

2.

for every
w\in V(G)
, the data structures representing
N_{L}(w)
and
N_{R}(w)
contain the vertices they should by the definition of these sets, and

3.

for every
w\in L
, the matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{w}
is a maximal matching between the vertices in
N_{R}(w)
and the vertices in
T^{2}_{L}(w)
,
and if
w\not\in\mathsf{Cand}
then also
|N_{L}(w)|+|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{w}|\geq p+1

The update will be perfomed by the Algorithm
1
, which in turn calls the Algorithm
2
presented later. For the time being, it is sufficient to know that the following lemma (which we prove after presenting
2
) holds:

Lemma 5
.

Given a vertex
v
as input,
2
returns FALSE if
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
is a maximum matching between
N_{R}(v)
and
T^{2}_{L}(v)
. Otherwise, it returns a new maxi
mal
matching between these sets with
|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}|+1
edges.

Algorithm 1

Updates the Oracle data structures when moving a vertex
v
from
L
to
R
.

Input:

A vertex
v
that was in

\mathsf{Cand}

1

\mathsf{Check}=\emptyset

//

1

Update neighbours in
N(v)

2

for

u\in N(v)

do

3

Remove
v
from
N_{L}(u)

4

Add
v
to
N_{R}(u)

//

2

Ensure maximality of affected matchings

5

for

u\in N_{L}(v)

do

6

for

w\in N_{L}(v)

do

7

if

w\not\in L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}\cup N_{L}(u)\cup\{u\}

then

8

Add the edge
vw
to
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}

9

break

10

Add
u
to
\mathsf{Check}

//

3

Update relevant neighbours in
T^{2}_{L}(v)

11

for

u\in\bigcup_{x\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}}N_{L}(x)\setminus\{v\}

do

12

if

v\not\in L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}

then

13

continue

14

Let
vz
be the edge in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
incident to
v

15

Remove
vz
from
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}

16

for

y\in N_{L}(z)

do

17

if

y\not\in N_{L}(u)\cup L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}\cup\{u\}

then

18

Add the edge
yz
to
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}

19

break

20

Add
u
to
\mathsf{Check}

//

4

Check whether affected vertices become candidates

21

for

u\in\mathsf{Check}

do

22

if

|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}|+|N_{L}(u)|=p
and
u\not\in\mathsf{Cand}

then

23

if

Augmenting
(u)
= FALSE

then

24

Add
u
to
\mathsf{Cand}

25

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}:=\emptyset

Let us now prove that the three conditions over the Oracle data structures hold after they are initialised and that they are maintained by
1
. This, by Lemma
4
, already implies that
Main
works correctly.

Afterwards we will bound the running time of
1
and the number of times that
1
executes
2
with the same input. Both lemmas are required in order to bound the running time of
Main
. After that, we prove Lemma
5
and bound the overall running time of
2
to then present the main theorem of this section.

Lemma 6
.

The first three data structure conditions hold after the Oracle data structure initialisation and before and after every time
1
is executed.

Proof.

Prior to the first time
1
is executed we have that
L=V(G)
. At this stage, for every
v\in V(G)
the matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
must be empty and
\operatorname{pp}^{2}_{L}(v)
is equal to the degree of
v
.
Therefore
\mathsf{Cand}
must contain every vertex that has degree at most
p
. This is precisely how we initialise the relevant data structures and we conclude that conditions (1)-(3) are met.

Let us now deal with maintaining Condition (2). The loop
in
1
updates the data structures

N_{R}
,
N_{L}
for all vertices in
N(v)
, where
v
is the vertex that was just moved from
L
to
R
. It is easy to see that these are the only vertices for which the values of
N_{R}
and
N_{L}
change, hence we conclude that Condition (2) is maintained by
1
. Moreover, we can assume that it holds when the parts
2
–
4
of
1
are executed.

Let us now deal with the Conditions (1) and (3). In order to talk about the state of various data structures in the algorithm, we will use left superscript annotations
i
,
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, to denote the state of a data structure
after
the execution of Loop
i
. Sets and data structures without annotation refer to their state before the execution of
1
. To avoid very convoluted
notation, we write e.g.

, a contradiction.
∎

As a corollary to this claim, we find that a matching

: Loop
1
does not modify any matchings. Loop
2
does not modify
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
since by assumption

and thus

, in particular
u
will not be added to
\mathsf{Check}
. Loop
3
will not modify
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
since
v\not\in V({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u})
, so if the loop considers
u
, then it continues in line 13 without modifying
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
and
u
is not added to
\mathsf{Check}
. Since no loop so far added
u
to
\mathsf{Check}
, we can conclude that Loop
4
never considers
u
and therefore does not modify
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
. We conclude that

.

Consider now a vertex
u\in L
with
v\in{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
. Since
v\in L
, this is only possible if
u\in T^{2}_{L}(v)
. Since the Oracle does not store this set, we need to justify line 11 by showing that
u\in\bigcup_{x\in R^{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}}N_{L}(x)
.

Claim 8
.

Assume
v\in V({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u})
. Then there exists a vertex
x\in R^{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
such that

u\in N_{L}(x)
.

Proof.

Since
v\in L
and
v\in V({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u})
we conclude that
v\in T^{2}_{L}(u)
. This of course also means that
u\in T^{2}_{L}(v)
, so clearly there exist at least one vertex
x\in N_{R}(v)
with
u\in N_{L}(x)
. If all such vertices were not contained in
x\in R^{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
, then clearly also
u\not\in L^{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
and we could add the edge
ux
to
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
, contradicting maximality.
∎

We conclude that Loop
3
will indeed consider
u
and, by assumption, reach line 14 in this iteration. Note that Loop
2
did not modify

. In summary, all vertices

Algorithm 2

Attempts to increase the local matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
for a given vertex
v
.

Input:

A vertex
v

Output:

TRUE
if the algorithm added one more edge to
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
,
FALSE
otherwise.

//
Construct auxiliary matching graph

1

Create an empty graph
\vec{H}_{v}
with vertices
V({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v})

2

S:=\emptyset
,
T:=\emptyset
,
\mathsf{out}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset

3

for

u\in N_{R}(v)

do

4

for

w\in L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}

do

5

if

uw\in E({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v})

then

6

Add directed edge
uw
to
E(\vec{H}_{v})

7

else if

uw\in E(G)

then

8

if

u\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}

then

9

Add directed edge
wu
to
E(\vec{H}_{v})

10

else

11

Add
w
to
T

12

\mathsf{out}(w)=u

13

for

u\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}

do

14

for

w\in N_{L}(u)

do

15

if

w\notin L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}\cup N_{L}(v)\cup\{v\}

then

16

Add
u
to
S

17

\mathsf{out}(u)=w

//
Augment matching if possible

18

if

there is a directed path
\vec{P}
from
S
to
T
in
\vec{H}_{v}

then

19

Let
s\in S
,
t\in T
be the endpoints of
\vec{P}

20

E^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=E(\vec{P})\setminus{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}\cup\{s\mathsf{out}(s),t\mathsf{out}(t)\}

21

E^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=E(\vec{P})\cap{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}

22

Remove
E^{-}
from
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
and add
E^{+}
to
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}

23

return
TRUE

24

else

//
Includes the case when one of
S,T
is empty

25

return
FALSE

Lemma 10
.

The total running time of
1
is
O(|G|p^{3})
plus the total running time of
2
.

Proof.

We assume that all basic data structures are implemented using hashing, and therefore all operations excluding loops take expected constant time.
Thus, to bound the total running time we only need to bound the number of times every loop is executed.

Loop
1
in
1
is over vertices
N(v)
. Since every vertex of
G

takes the role of
v
precisely once, this contributes
O(\|G\|)=O(p|G|)
to the total running time.

Loop
2
iterates over the vertices of
N_{L}(v)
.
Since
v\in\mathsf{Cand}
, and Condition (1) for the data structures holds by Lemma
6
, we know that
\operatorname{pp}_{L}(v)\leq p
and in particular
(by Lemma
2
)
|N_{L}(v)|\leq p
. Consequently the running time of the Loop
2
is
O(p^{2})
.

Loop
3
iterates over
\bigcup_{x\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}}N_{L}(x)
and it has an internal loop over the vertices of
N_{L}(z)
.
Since
\operatorname{pp}_{L}(v)\leq p
, by Lemma
2
,
|R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}|\leq p
.
As every vertex in
R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
is in
R
and therefore was at some earlier point in
\mathsf{Cand}
we have, by the same reasoning as for
v
, that
|N_{L}(x)|\leq p
, for every
x\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
, and therefore
|\bigcup_{x\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}}N_{L}(x)|\leq p^{2}
.
We note that prior to the removal of
vz
from
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
it held that
v\in L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}
and hence
z\in R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}
. So
z\in R
which again means that
|N_{L}(z)|\leq p
for every
z
using during the loop.
Consequently, the total running time of Loop
3
is
O(p^{3})
.

Finally, Loop
4
iterates over all the vertices in
\mathsf{Check}
.
Since Loop
2
and
3
add at most one vertex per iteration to
\mathsf{Check}
, we have that
|\mathsf{Check}|=O(p^{3})
and therefore we have that many iterations of Loop
4
.

Finally, since in a full execution of
Main
,
1
is called exactly once for every vertex in
G
the overall running time of
1
is
O(|G|p^{3})
plus the total running time of all calls to
2
. ∎

Lemma 11
.

In a full execution of

.
By Lemma
6
, this can only happen if
|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}|+|N_{L}(u)|=p+1
and either (i) Loop
1
reduced
N_{L}(u)
and Loop
2
did not add an edge to
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
, or (ii) Loop
3
removed an edge from
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
and did not add another one. We now show that (i) and (ii) can happen at most
O(p^{2})
times for
u
during a full run of
Main
.

Consider the first execution of
1
where
2
(
u
) was called and let us call this point in time
\star
. Since
|{}^{\star}{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}|+|{}^{\star}N_{L}(u)|=p
we know that
|{}^{\star}N_{L}(u)|\leq p
.
We further have that
{}^{\scalebox{.55}{\hbox to11.24pt{\vbox to11.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.61992pt\lower-5.61992pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#808080} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#808080} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.5 0 C 7.5 4.14 4.14 7.5 0 7.5 C -4.14 7.5 -7.5 4.14 -7.5 0 C -7.5 -4.14 -4.14 -7.5 0 -7.5 C 4.14 -7.5 7.5 -4.14 7.5 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.34029pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.62 -3.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{71}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}^{\star}N_{L}(u)\subseteq{}^{\star}N_{L}(u)
so this bound holds for the rest of the run of
Main
. Therefore, (i) can only happen
|{}^{\star}N_{L}(u)|\leq p
many
times. Now note that every time (ii) happens, the Loop
3
removes an edge from the current matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}
and in particular a vertex from the
current
L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}
. We will now bound the number of vertices that can from
\star
onwards appear in
T^{2}_{L}(u)
, and therefore in
L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}
, which then bound the number of times (ii) can happen.

First, let us bound the number of vertices in
{}^{\star}T^{2}_{L}(u)
. Since
{}^{\star}M_{u}
is a maximal matching between
{}^{\star}N_{R}(u)
and
{}^{\star}T^{2}_{L}(u)
it holds that
{}^{\star}T^{2}_{L}(u)\subseteq\bigcup_{x\in{}^{\star}R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}}N_{L}(x)
.
Therefore,
|{}^{\star}T^{2}_{L}(u)|\leq p|{}^{\star}R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}|
as every vertex in
{}^{\star}R
has at most
p
neighbours in
{}^{\star}L
. As established above,
|{}^{\star}{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{u}|\leq p
therefore
|{}^{\star}T^{2}_{L}(u)|\leq p^{2}
.

Now note that if some vertex
z
is added to
T^{2}_{L}(u)
at some point after
\star
, then it is because some vertex
v\in N_{L}(u)
with
z\in N_{L}(v)
was moved from
L
to
R
. At this point,
|N_{L}(v)|\leq p
so
v
adds at most
p
vertices to
T^{2}_{L}(u)
. Finally note that this can happen at most
p
times since
|{}^{\star}N_{L}(u)|\leq p
and this set can only decrease in size. In total, these occurrences after
\star
add at most
p^{2}
further vertices to
T^{2}_{L}(u)
.

We conclude that in total at most
O(p^{2})
vertices are added to
T^{2}_{L}(u)

after
\star
and therefore at most that many vertices can appear in
L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{u}
. Thus (ii) can only happen
O(p^{2})
times in total which proves the claim.
∎

See
5

Proof.

Let
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
be the matching before
2
(
v
) is executed and
{}^{\star}{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
afterwards. As shown in the the proof of Lemma
6
, when
2
(
v
) is called, the matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
is maximal with at most
p
edges.

Let
H_{v}
be the bipartite graph with sides
N_{R}(v)
,
T^{2}_{L}(v)
and all edges between these sets that exist in
G
. If
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
is not maximum, then there exists an augmenting path
P
in the
H_{v}
which starts in
T^{2}_{L}(u)\setminus V({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v})
, goes through
H_{v}[{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}]
with alternating matching and non-matching edges, and ends in
N_{R}(v)\setminus V({\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v})
.

Let
\vec{H}_{v}
,
S\subseteq N_{R}(u)
,
T\subseteq T^{2}_{L}(v)
, and
\mathsf{out}
be as constructed by
2
. Let us first show how
\vec{H}_{v}
relates to
H_{v}
:

Claim 12
.

The bipartite graph
\vec{H}_{v}[{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}]
is an orientation of
H_{v}[{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}]
where the edges
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
go from
N_{R}(v)
to
T^{2}_{L}(v)
and all other edges go in the other direction.

Proof.

The loops in line 4 and line 5 iterate over all pairs
u\in N_{R}(v)
,
w\in L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
and add either the arc
uw
to
\vec{H}_{v}
if
uw\in E(M_{v})
or the arc
wu
if
uw\in E(G)\setminus E(M_{v})
. Therefore all edges in
H_{v}[{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}]
appear in
\vec{H}_{v}
.
∎

We argue that if an augmenting path
P
exists in
H_{v}
, then there exists a directed
path
\vec{P}
from
S
to
T
in
\vec{H}_{v}
. To that end, let
P=asQtb
,
with
a\in T^{2}_{L}(v)
,
sQt\subseteq V(M_{v})
, and
b\in N_{R}(v)
. Since
a\in N_{R}(s)
and
b\in N_{L}(t)
, we can conclude that
\mathsf{out}(s)
and
\mathsf{out}(t)
exist and that
s\in S
and
t\in T
.
Since the edges in
sQt
must alternate between matching and non-matching edges, it is easy to verify that
sQt
is indeed a directed path in
\vec{H}_{v}
.
Accordingly, if
2
returns FALSE the matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
does not have an augmenting path in
H_{v}
and is therefore a maximum matching.

Let us now argue that for any directed path
\vec{P}=s\vec{Q}t
from
S
to
T
in
\vec{H}_{v}
it holds that the path
\mathsf{out}(s)s\vec{Q}t\mathsf{out}(t)
is an augmenting path for
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
in
H_{v}
. By construction of
\vec{H}_{v}
, every vertex in
N_{R}(v)
has exactly one outgoing edge, namely the matching edge. Therefore, a directed path from
S\subseteq N_{R}(v)
to
T\subseteq T^{2}_{L}(v)
must alternative between matching and non-matching edges. Since
\mathsf{out}(s)s

and
t\mathsf{out}(t)
are edges outside of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
, we conclude that
\mathsf{out}(s)s\vec{Q}t\mathsf{out}(t)
is indeed an augmenting path for
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
in
H_{v}
. Lines
20
to
24
perform the usual augmentation of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
using the edges of this path, therefore we conclude that
2
indeed returns a matching
{}^{\star}M_{v}
of size
|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}|+1
. The maximality of
{}^{\star}M_{v}
simply follows from the maximality of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
.
∎

Lemma 13
.

The total runtime of
2
in a full execution of
Main
is
O(p^{4}|G|)
.

Proof.

We note that since we use hashes for the data structures, all the operations excluding loops take constant time.
Thus, to bound the total running time we only need to bound the number of times every loop is executed over all calls to
2
.

The first loop in
2
is over the vertices in
N_{R}(v)
and it has an internal loop over the vertices of
L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
.
According to
1
, when the algorithm is executed for
v
,
|L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}|\leq p
.
By the second condition over the data structures,
N_{R}(v)\subseteq N(v)
.
According to Lemma
11
,
2
is executed at most
O(p^{2})
times with
v
as an input.

So, over a full execution of
Main
, the total running time of the first loop is

\sum_{v\in V(G)}O(p^{2})|N(v)|p=O(p^{3})\cdot 2\|G\|=O(p^{4}|G|).

The second loop in
2
is over the vertices in
R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
and it has an internal loop over the vertices of
N_{L}(v)
.
Recall that
1
calls
2
(
v
) when
|N_{L}(v)|+|R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}|\leq p
.
So, by a similar computation to the above, over a full execution of
Main
the total running time of the second loop is
O(p^{4}|G|)
.

Finally, the conditional statement at line 19 of
2
requires the computation of a directed path. By executing a simple DFS from
S
to
T

in
\vec{H}_{v}
in time
O(\|\vec{H}_{v}\|)=O(p^{2})
. The computation of these paths therefore also contributed a total running time of
O(p^{4}|G|)
and the claim follows.
∎

Theorem 14
.

On input graph
G
and input parameter
p
,
the total runtime of
Main
is
O(p^{4}|G|)
, the total space used
Main
is
O(\|G\|+p^{2})
,
and the algorithm returns FALSE if
\operatorname{adm}_{2}(G)>p
and otherwise it returns an ordering
<
such that
\operatorname{adm}_{2}(G,<)\leq p
of the vertices of
G
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
10
, the overall running of
1
is
O(p^{3}|G|)
plus the total running time of
2
.
According to Lemma
13
, the total running time of all the executions of
2
is
O(p^{4}|G|)
.
The operations of
Main
do not add more time and we arrive at the claimed running time of
O(p^{4}|G|)
.

To compute the space, recall that the Oracle data structure consists of

•

\mathsf{Cand}
, a subset of
V(G)
, and hence of size at most
|G|
.

•

N_{L}(v)
and
N_{R}(v)
for every
v\in V(G)
, so overall order of
\sum_{v\in V(G)}|N_{L}(v)|+|N_{R}(v)|=\sum_{v\in V(G)}|N(v)|=\|G\|
, and

•

A matching
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}
, between the vertices of
N_{R}(v)
and some other vertices, thus, overall
\sum_{v\in V(G)}|N_{R}(v)|\leq\sum_{v\in V(G)}|N(v)|=\|G\|
.

Beyond this, the routine
2
when executed for a vertex
v

constructs a directed graph between the vertices of
R^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
and
L^{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}}_{v}
. Since
1
ensures this only happens when
|{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\hbox{$\mathsf{M}$}}{\mathsf{M}}{\mathsf{M}}}_{v}|=O(p)
,
we can conclude that
2
uses at most
O(p^{2})
space.
Consequently the total space used by
Main
is
O(p^{2}+\|G\|)
.

Finally, to prove the correctness of the algorithm, recall that
by Lemma
4
the
2
-admissibility of the input graph
is decided correctly if every execution of
1
returns a vertex
v

such that
\operatorname{pp}_{L}(v)\leq p
if such a vertex exists and otherwise return FALSE. Lemma
5
proves that
2
works as claimed,
accordingly Lemma
6
then shows that
Main
works as claimed.
∎

5
Implementation details and experiments

We implemented the algorithm in Rust v1.75 and computed the
2
-admissibility of 214 real-world networks. The full results are available in the repository, we present here a summary of our findings.
The dataset contains networks from various domains, like
biology (protein-protein interaction networks like
BioGrid-Homo-Sapiens
, neural networks like
bn-mouse_retina_1
);
infrastructure networks
(
autobahn
,
euroroad
,
exnet-water
);
social networks (
livemocha
,
soc-gplus
,
twittercrawl
);
dependency graphs (
JDK_dependency
,
linux
);
web crawls (
web-EPA
,
web-california
);
citation and co-author networks (
cit-HepPh
,
ca-HepPh
,
zewail
);
digital communication networks (
email-Enron
);
word networks (
foldoc
,
edinburgh_associative_thesaurus
);
and ownership networks (
bahamas
,
offshore
).

All experiments were run with an AMD Ryzen R1600 @ 2.6 GHz and 8GB of RAM.
Since the algorithm is designed to solve the decision variant, we compute the
2
-admissibility using binary search. We measured memory consumption by running separate experiments with a modified memory allocator that records peak allocation. The variation of both running times and peak memory were small, we therefore report results of single runs here and not averages over multiple runs.

Figure 1
:
Running time dependence on
2
-admissibility. Marker sizes represent the number of edges. The dotted lines and percentages indicate on how many networks the algorithm finished below the specified time.

Figure 2
:
Peak memory consumption dependence on
2
-admissibility. Marker sizes represent the number of edges. The dotted lines and percentages indicate on how many networks the algorithm used less than the indicate amount of memory. On average, a third of the memory consumption is due to storing the network.

The algorithm is resource-efficient

For the running time (Figure
1
) we find that the algorithm performs well for networks whose
2
-admissibility lies below
\sim
300, in this range the network size is the dominating factor. This indicates that the worst-case of
O(p^{4}|G|)
is quite pessimistic and therefore that the ‘optimistic’ algorithm design paid off. The computation took less than ten minutes on almost all networks (96%), including some with millions of edges, and finished in less than a second for most smaller networks (71%).

Memory consumption was, as predicted by theory, modest (Figure
2
). None of our experiments used more than 1.34 GB of RAM. Note that already simply storing the networks in-memory accounts for a good fraction of the total memory usage: Among the 44 networks with noticable memory footprint (¿ 10MB), on average 30% of memory accounts for storing the network itself and 70% is used by the algorithm’s data structures.

The
2
-admissibility is small in many real-world graphs

Figure 3
:

Comparison of
2
-admissibility and degeneracy. Marker sizes represent the number of edges. The line represents a best-fit polynomial
\operatorname{adm}_{2}\approx\deg^{1.25}
, the red markers are outliers whose residual has an
absolute z-score
\geq 3
.

We find that 25% of networks have a
2
-admissibility of less than
5
and 50% have a
2
-admissibility of
16
or less (the latter includes networks with over half a million edges). This means that even algorithms that have an exponential dependence on the
2
-admissibility could be useful on these instances.

As networks vary in density, we compare the
2
-admissibility relative to the degeneracy of the networks to obtain a more unified view. Let
d
be the degeneracy (the independent variable) and
a_{2}
the
2
-admissibility (the dependent variable) of the networks. We fitted the functions
a_{2}=\alpha\dot{d}
,
a_{2}=d^{\alpha}
, and
a_{2}=\alpha^{d}
with parameter
\alpha
using
numpy
’s non-linear least squares optimisation, with the following results:

Linear

Polynomial

Exponential

Best fit

3.045\cdot d

d^{1.2477}

1.0475^{d}

R^{2}

0.676

0.693

0.484273

Slightly more complicated functions (like
\alpha d^{\beta}+\gamma
) did not result in a significantly better fit and we chose to keep the modelling
simple. Hence, we propose that the
2
-admissibility grows roughly as
d^{1.25}
(
cf
. Figure
3
), as such we expect most networks with low degeneracy to also have low
2
-admissibility

We find six outlier networks with this model by computing the
residual Z-scores.
ca-HepPh
,
mousebrain
, and
twittercrawl

have a residual Z-score
\leq-3
, therefore their
2
-admissibility is lower than expected;
dogster_friendships
,
livemocha
, and

tv_tropes
have a residual Z-score
\geq 3
, therefore their

2
-admissibility is higher than expected. One possible reason is that the
first three networks have a relatively high clustering coefficient
7
7

7

The clustering coefficient of a vertex is the number of triangles it is part of divided by the number of pairs in its neighbourhood; the clustering coefficient of a graph is the average over these values.
(0.61, 0.76, and 0.26, respectively) compared to the latter three (0.17, 0.05, and 0.0, respectively), we leave the investigation of this relationship for future work.

6
Conclusion

We demonstrated that careful algorithm design and an ‘optimistic’ approach resulted in a resource-efficient implementation to compute the
2
-admissibility of real-world graphs. Our experiments not only show that the implementation is of practical use, but also that the
2
-admissibility is indeed a measure that is reasonably small on most practical instances.

In the future, we want to take our learnings from this algorithm and apply it to implement algorithms for
3
- and
4
-admissibility, as these measures might provide further insight into network structure and might still be tractable in practice.

7
